Desingularization of vortex sheets for the 2D Euler equations
Alberto Enciso, Antonio J. Fernández, David Meyer

Abstract
. We show how to regularize vortex sheets by means of smooth, compactly supported vorticities that asymptotically evolve according to the Birkhoff–Rott vortex sheet dynamics. More precisely, consider a vortex sheet initial datum ω sing 0 \omega^{0}_{\mathrm{sing}} , which is a signed Radon measure supported on a closed curve. We construct a family of initial vorticities ω ε 0 ∈ C c ∞ ​ ( ℝ 2 ) \omega^{0}_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) converging to ω sing 0 \omega^{0}_{\mathrm{sing}} distributionally as ε → 0 + \varepsilon\to 0^{+} , and show that the corresponding solutions ω ε ​ ( x , t ) \omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t) to the 2D incompressible Euler equations converge to the measure defined by the Birkhoff–Rott system with initial datum ω sing 0 \omega^{0}_{\mathrm{sing}} . The regularization relies on a layer construction designed to exploit the key observation that the Kelvin–Helmholtz instability has a strongly anisotropic effect: while vorticities must be analytic in the “tangential” direction, the way layers can be arranged in the “normal” direction is essentially arbitrary.



1.
Introduction

The 2D incompressible Euler equations describe the movement of ideal fluids on the plane. In vorticity form, this system of equations can be written as

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \ &\partial_{t}\omega+u\cdot\nabla\omega=0\quad&&\textup{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,T)\,,\\
&u:=K*\omega&&\textup{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T)\,,\\
&\omega(\cdot,0)=\omega_{0}&&\textup{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\,,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\omega:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}
is the vorticity of the fluid, and
u:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the corresponding velocity field, which is linked to the vorticity through the Biot–Savart law. Here,
\omega_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is the initial value of the vorticity and

K(x):=\frac{x^{\perp}}{2\pi|x|^{2}}\,,\quad x^{\perp}:=(-x_{2},x_{1})\,,

denotes the Biot–Savart kernel, so that the velocity field
u
is divergence-free and
\nabla^{\perp}\cdot u=\omega
.

In many physical situations, the vorticity tends to concentrate on a lower dimensional subset of physical space. Consequently, a classical topic in the study of the incompressible Euler equations is the analysis of solutions whose vorticity is concentrated on small sets, and the justification of effective models for these situations. The simplest case is that of vorticities supported on
N
planar domains with small diameter. By letting these domains shrink down to points
\xi_{1}^{0},\dots,\xi_{N}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, one formally arrives at the
N
-vortex model
. This model posits that the evolution of the limit vorticity, which is the collection of deltas

(1.2)

\omega^{0}_{N,\,\mathrm{sing}}(x):=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\varpi_{n}\,\delta(x-\xi_{n}^{0})\,,

with
\varpi_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
,
should be morally given by

\omega_{N,\,\mathrm{sing}}(x,t):=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\varpi_{n}\,\delta(x-\xi_{n}(t))\,.

Here
(\xi_{n})_{n=1}^{N}:[0,T_{0})\to\mathbb{R}^{2N}
is the only solution to the Kirchhoff–Routh system of ODEs

(1.3)

\dot{\xi}_{n}(t)=\sum_{m\neq n}\varpi_{m}\,K(\xi_{m}(t)-\xi_{n}(t))\,,\qquad\xi_{n}(0)=\xi_{n}^{0}\,,\qquad n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\,,

which is defined for some
T_{0}>0
.

To connect these heuristic considerations with actual Euler flows, one proceeds by desingularizing the system of
N
point vortices. To this end, let us fix an arbitrary cutoff function, say
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, which we normalize so that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
. Using that the function
\chi_{\varepsilon}(x):=\varepsilon^{-2}\chi(x/\varepsilon)
converges weakly to the delta distribution as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, one can consider the following regularization of (
1.2
):

(1.4)

\omega^{0}_{N,\varepsilon}(x):=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\varpi_{n}\,\chi_{\varepsilon}(x-\xi_{n}^{0})\,.

It is standard that
\omega^{0}_{N,\varepsilon}\to\omega_{N,\,\mathrm{sing}}
in the sense of distributions as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, and that the Euler equations (
1.1
) admit a unique global solution with initial datum (
1.4
), which we denote by
\omega_{N,\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty))
. However, the well-posedness theory for the Euler equations does not provide any a priori information on the structure of the solution.

A rigorous connection between Euler flows and the
N
-vortex model (
1.3
) was first established in two classical results of Marchioro and Pulverenti
[
30
,
31
]
. Their main result in
[
31
]
ensures that there exists some time
T>0
, independent of the small parameter
\varepsilon
, such that the unique Euler flow with initial datum (
1.4
),
\omega_{N,\varepsilon}
, satisfies

\omega_{N,\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\to\omega_{N,\,\mathrm{sing}}(\cdot,t)

in the sense of distributions as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, for all
t\in[0,T)
. With a suitably chosen cutoff, much more precise estimates for the convergence of
\omega_{\varepsilon}
to
\omega_{\mathrm{sing}}
were derived by Dávila, del Pino, Musso and Wei in
[
10
]
using gluing techniques. Vortex desingularization problems, both for the incompressible Euler equations and for related models such as the Navier–Stokes equations, have garnered significant attention in recent years; see, for example,
[
29
,
20
,
14
,
7
,
11
,
12
,
24
]
.

An important yet still insufficiently understood class of solutions with concentrated vorticity are vortex sheets. These are Euler flows whose vorticity is concentrated on a curve, which corresponds to considering an initial vorticity given by a measure of the form

(1.5)

\omega^{0}_{\mathrm{sing}}(x):=\varpi^{0}(s)\,\delta(x-\Gamma(s))\displaystyle\,.

Here
\Gamma(s)
is a sufficiently smooth curve, and the vorticity amplitude
\varpi^{0}(s)
is a smooth function.

A well-known formal computation suggests that the evolution of this vorticity should morally be of the form

(1.6)

\omega_{\mathrm{sing}}(x,t):=\varpi(s,t)\,\delta(x-\gamma(s,t))\displaystyle\,,

where the curve
\gamma(\cdot,t)
and the vorticity amplitude
\varpi(\cdot,t)
at time
t
are obtained by solving the Birkhoff–Rott equations

(1.7)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\gamma(s,t)+c(s,t)\partial_{s}\gamma(s,t)={\rm BR}[\gamma;\,\varpi](s,t)\,,\\
&\partial_{t}\varpi(s,t)+\partial_{s}(c(s,t)\varpi(s,t))=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

with initial conditions

\gamma(\cdot,0)=\Gamma\,,\quad\textup{ and }\quad\varpi(\cdot,0)=\varpi^{0}\,.

Here

(1.8)

{\rm BR}[\gamma;\,\varpi](s,t):={\rm p.v.}\int_{s_{0}}^{s_{1}}K(\gamma(s,t)-\gamma(\varsigma,t))\varpi(\varsigma,t){\,\mathrm{d}}\varsigma

is the Birkhoff–Rott operator,
[s_{0},s_{1}]
is the interval on which the curve is defined, and
c(s,t)
is an arbitrary fixed function whose choice is immaterial. Indeed, the appearance of the function
c
is associated with the fact that the measure (
1.6
) does not depend on how one parametrizes the curve (and the vorticity density), and, in a natural way, solutions to the Birkhoff–Rott equations with different
c
give rise to the same measure.
Lopes Filho, Nussenzveig and Schochet
[
28
]
have recently shown that, under minimal assumptions, the vorticity defined by (
1.6
) is a weak solution to the Euler equations (
1.1
) if and only if
(\gamma,\varpi)
satisfies (
1.7
).

The well-posedness theory for vortex sheets is remarkably hard. This is mainly due to the fact that they are subject to Kelvin–Helmholtz instability
[
34
]
, so that higher Fourier modes of a small perturbation of a flat profile can get exponentially amplified in the evolution. While the Birkhoff–Rott equations are ill-posed on Sobolev spaces, as shown by Caflisch and Orellana in the seminal paper
[
4
]
, they are locally well-posed on analytic spaces
[
38
]
. This can be heuristically understood as a consequence of the fact that the Birkhoff–Rott equations are elliptic in nature, a property that Lebeau
[
27
]
and Wu
[
40
]
have exploited to show that, under suitable assumptions, solutions to (
1.7
) become analytic instantaneously. It is known
[
4
]
that analytic initial data can develop singularities in finite time. The important case of self-similar spirals (see
[
36
, Chapter 8]
) was analyzed in detail in
[
37
,
25
,
8
]
. In addition, various special types of solutions, such as translating and rotating sheets, have been constructed in
[
5
,
6
,
9
,
21
,
33
]
.

From the perspective of the 2D Euler equations, a celebrated result of Delort
[
13
]
ensures the existence of weak solutions to (
1.1
) with initial datum of the form (
1.5
), provided that the vorticity density
\varpi^{0}
does not change sign.
However, even with this distinguished sign assumption, the uniqueness and structure of such solutions remain unknown. On the other hand, in the framework of Euler flows whose vorticity is no longer a measure, Székelyhidi
[
39
]
used convex integration techniques to show that there exist infinitely many bounded weak solutions to (
1.1
) with a specific flat vortex sheet as initial data. Further developments are given in
[
32
,
15
]
.

In this paper, we are concerned with the dynamics of regularized vortex sheets and their connection with the Birkhoff–Rott equations. As a consequence of the aforementioned analytic subtleties, the situation here is much less clear-cut than in the case of point vortices, and in fact, there are only two rigorous results on this topic. To analyze the vortex sheets, both impose periodic boundary conditions in the first coordinate (i.e.,
x\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
) and assume that the curve
\Gamma(s):=(s,h_{\Gamma}(s))
is a graph.

First, a classical result due to Benedetto and Pulvirenti
[
2
]
considers the evolution of a thin, almost flat vortex layer of constant vorticity. This corresponds to taking a patch-type vorticity of the form
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0,\mathrm{BP}}(x):=\varepsilon^{-1}\,\mathds{1}_{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}}(x)
, where

\Lambda_{\varepsilon}:=\Big\{x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}:|x_{2}-h_{\Gamma}(x_{1})|<\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon\varpi^{0}(x_{1})\Big\}\,,

is a small tubular neighborhood of the curve
\Gamma
whose width, of order
\varepsilon
, is proportional to the vorticity amplitude. Since the initial vorticity is bounded and compactly supported, the Euler equations with this datum have a unique solution
\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
by the Yudovich theory
[
41
]
. The authors show that, if
(\Gamma,\varpi)
is a small analytic perturbation of a flat sheet of constant vorticity (i.e, the functions
\varpi^{0}
and
h_{\Gamma}
are analytic and close enough to 1), then there exists some positive time
T
independent of
\varepsilon
such that, for all
t\in[0,T)
,

\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{BP}}(\cdot,t)\to\omega_{\mathrm{sing}}(\cdot,t)\,,

in the sense of distribution, as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. Note that the Birkhoff–Rott evolution (
1.7
) does determine
(\gamma,\varpi)
for some positive time because the initial data is analytic. A drawback of this construction is that the regularized vorticity is discontinuous. It is not obvious a priori how to adapt this approach to achieve higher regularity; in fact, the authors explicitly point out that the construction should
not
work if one replaces the indicator function by a general cutoff function
[
2
, Section 4]
.

The second result, recently obtained by Caflisch, Lombardo, and Sammartino
[
3
]
, considers the setting of reasonably small analytic perturbations of a flat sheet, where the slope satisfies certain quantitative bounds. In this paper, the regularized vorticity is no longer an indicator function; instead, these authors develop a functional setting which enables them to consider analytic vorticities
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0,{\mathrm{CLS}}}
that decay exponentially away from
\Gamma
with a decay length of order
\varepsilon
. Hence, the vorticity does not have compact support, but it is smooth and sharply peaked around the curve
\Gamma
for small
\varepsilon
. The central result of their paper establishes a similar convergence result in this setting. To control the exponential decay of the solutions, the fact that
\Gamma
is a horizontal graph on the cylinder
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
is crucially used.

Our objective in this paper is to achieve the regularization of vortex sheets by smooth, compactly supported data on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, in a manner that applies to sheets that are not necessarily graphs. Consequently, we let
\Gamma:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be an analytic planar curve parametrized by arclength, and
\varpi^{0}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
be any analytic function. The fact that these functions are defined on
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
instead of on an open interval simply reflects that we want the curve
\Gamma
to be closed. Without any loss of generality, we assume that
\Gamma
has unit length.

To state our main result, let us denote by
\dot{\Gamma}(s)
the derivative of
\Gamma(s)
with respect to the arclength parameter
s
.
For the sake of concreteness
1
1

1

Although throughout the paper we will consider significantly more general initial vorticities of the form
\omega^{0}_{\varepsilon}(x):=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s)\,\delta(x-\Gamma(s)-\varepsilon(l+\eta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(s))\dot{\Gamma}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}l
, which offers greater flexibility for constructing solutions, the simpler choice of initial vorticity considered in the introduction suffices to present the key features of our construction.
, consider the initial vorticity

(1.9)

\omega^{0}_{\varepsilon}(x):=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varpi^{0}(s)\,\chi(l)\,\delta\big(x-\Gamma(s)-\varepsilon l\dot{\Gamma}(s)^{\perp}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}l\,,

where
\varepsilon
is a small positive constant.
Assuming that
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}((-1,1))
is a nonnegative cutoff function, normalized so that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(l)\,\mathrm{d}l=1
, it is not hard to see that
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, that its support is contained a tubular neighborhood around the curve
\Gamma
of width
\varepsilon
, and that
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}\to\omega^{0}_{\mathrm{sing}}
in the sense of distributions as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
.

Our main result asserts that the regularization of vortex sheets given by (
1.9
) (or, indeed, by the considerably more general expressions discussed in Section
2
) does possess all the properties that one may desire. A somewhat informal statement of this fact is the following; for a precise, more general statement, see Theorem
2.1
. It is worth mentioning that a minor modification of the proof (which amounts to replacing the Birkhoff–Rott operator (
1.8
) by its periodic counterpart) yields an analogous result for the Euler equations on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
or on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider the initial vorticities
\omega^{0}_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
given by (
1.9
), which converge distributionally to the vortex sheet (
1.5
) as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, and let
\omega_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,\infty))
denote the unique solution to the Euler equations (
1.1
) with initial datum
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}
.

If the curve
\Gamma
and the initial vorticity density
\varpi^{0}
are analytic, there exists some time
T>0
, which does not depend on
\varepsilon
, such that, for all
t\in(0,T)
,
\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
converges distributionally as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
to the singular measure
\omega_{\mathrm{sing}}(\cdot,t)
defined by (
1.6
) in terms of the solution to the Birkhoff–Rott equations (
1.7
) with initial datum
(\Gamma,\varpi^{0})
.

The key insight underlying our construction is that the instability induced by the Birkhoff–Rott equations affects the dynamics of regularized vortex sheets in a highly anisotropic manner. Roughly speaking, the mechanism at play is as follows. Writing the regularized initial datum in terms of a continuum of parallel sheets, we need to impose that all these sheets are analytic to establish a relation with the Birkhoff–Rott equations, which are only locally well-posed on analytic spaces. However, it turns out that one can get by with much less regularity in the normal direction, as suggested by the result of Benedetto and Pulverenti
[
2
]
. As we will see, this distinction between the tangential and normal regularity is essential and remains valid for vorticities much more general than that in (
1.9
). Interestingly, a related phenomenon was already encountered by Fefferman and Rodrigo’s analysis of the lifespan of almost-sharp fronts for the SQG equations
[
16
,
17
,
18
]
, and a somewhat similar anisotropic regularity effect is a key idea in
[
18
]
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce the geometric and functional setting we will use, and state our main result, namely Theorem
2.1
. Then, in Section
3
, we present the proof of Theorem
2.1
. To streamline the presentation, the proofs of most results stated there are postponed to later sections. Specifically, in Section
4
, we derive an effective system of evolution equations used to obtain the family of solutions we seek. The existence of this family is established using Nishida’s Cauchy–Kovalevskaya theorem. The key estimates required for applying this theorem are proved in Sections
5
and
6
. First, in Section
5
, we prove the necessary geometric estimates; then, in Section
6
, we analyze the kernel appearing in the effective system of equations. Finally, in Section
7
, we address the results that imply convergence to the solution of the Birkhoff–Rott equations.

2.
Geometric setup and main result

For
\rho>0
, we set
\mathbb{T}_{\rho}:=(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\times[-\rho,\rho]
and denote by
{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbb{T}_{\rho})
the space of holomorphic functions
f
on the strip

\big\{z=s+i\beta\in\mathbb{C}:s\in\mathbb{R},\;-\rho\leqslant\beta\leqslant\rho\big\}\,,

satisfying the periodicity condition

f(z)=f(z+1)\,.

We denote by

{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho}:=\big\{f\in{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbb{T}_{\rho}):f(\mathbb{T})\subset\mathbb{R}\big\}\,,

the space of holomorphic functions
f:\mathbb{T}_{\rho}\to\mathbb{C}
that arise as complexifications of real-valued analytic functions on
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. We regard
{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho}
as a Banach space, equipped with the norm

(2.1)

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\rho}:=\sup_{|\beta|\leqslant\rho}\left\lVert f(\cdot+i\beta)\right\rVert_{C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})}.

We fix some non-intersecting closed planar curve, parametrized by arc-length. Rescaling it if necessary, without loss of generality, we can assume that the curve
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})
has unit length, which yields a map
\Gamma:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We suppose that the curve is analytic, so its complexification, which we still denote by
\Gamma
, is in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}^{2}
for some fixed
\rho_{0}>0
. For convenience, we can assume that
\Gamma^{(5)}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}^{2}
. We also assume that none of the (
\mathbb{C}^{2}
-valued) curves
\Gamma(\cdot+i\beta)
is self-intersecting for
|\beta|\leqslant\rho_{0}
. In fact, for technical reasons, without any loss of generality, we can assume the stronger condition that for every
\delta>0
we have

(2.2)

\displaystyle\min_{|\beta|\leqslant\rho_{0},\,|s-\alpha|\geqslant\delta}\,\operatorname{Re}\left\{\left[\Gamma_{1}(s+i\beta)-\Gamma_{1}(\alpha+i\beta)\right]^{2}+\left[\Gamma_{2}(s+i\beta)-\Gamma_{2}(\alpha+i\beta)\right]^{2}\right\}>0\,.

Indeed, by continuity and the fact that
\Gamma|_{\mathbb{T}}
does not self-intersect, this condition always holds provided that
\rho_{0}
is sufficiently small. Let us also recall that, possibly after reducing
\rho_{0}>0
, reparametrizing a curve by its arc-length preserves holomorphy, provided that
\dot{\Gamma}\neq 0
at every point.

Next, observe that there is a neighborhood of the graph of
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
where every point
x
can be uniquely written as

(2.3)

x=\Gamma(s)+n\dot{\Gamma}(s)^{\perp}\,.

More precisely, by the analytic inverse function theorem
[
26
, Theorem 2.5.3]
, there is some constant
R_{0}>0
and some tubular neighborhood
U_{\Gamma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the curve
\Gamma
such that (
2.3
) defines an analytic diffeomorphism

\mathbb{T}\times(-R_{0},R_{0})\ni(s,n)\mapsto x\in U_{\Gamma}\,.

We will denote this diffeomorphism and its inverse by

(s,n)\mapsto\mathbf{x}(s,n)\qquad\text{and}\qquad x\mapsto(\mathbf{s}(x),\mathbf{n}(x))\,,

respectively. Note that

(2.4)

\displaystyle e_{\mathbf{s}}(x):=\frac{\nabla\mathbf{s}(x)}{|\nabla\mathbf{s}(x)|^{2}}\,,\quad e_{\mathbf{n}}(x):=\nabla\mathbf{n}(x)\,,

is an analytic orthogonal (but not orthonormal) frame at every point
x\in U_{\Gamma}
. Since
|\nabla\mathbf{n}|=1
, we have chosen the normalization factors so that

e_{\mathbf{s}}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{s}=e_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{n}=1\quad\text{and}\quad e_{\mathbf{s}}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{n}=e_{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{s}=0\qquad\textup{in }U_{\Gamma}\,.

As the curve
\Gamma
is analytic, the vector fields
e_{\mathbf{s}},e_{\mathbf{n}}
can be uniquely extended to complex-valued fields in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}^{2}
, which we still denote by
e_{\mathbf{s}},e_{\mathbf{n}}
.
Note as well that, for every
x\in U_{\Gamma}
,

(2.5)

\displaystyle e_{\mathbf{n}}(x)=\dot{\Gamma}(\mathbf{s}(x))^{\perp}\qquad\text{and}\qquad e_{\mathbf{s}}(x)=\dot{\Gamma}(\mathbf{s}(x))+\mathbf{n}(x)\ddot{\Gamma}(\mathbf{s}(x))^{\perp},

as can be seen by differentiating (
2.3
) and taking the dot product with
\dot{\Gamma}(\mathbf{s}(x))^{\perp}
and
\dot{\Gamma}(\mathbf{s}(x))+\mathbf{n}(x)\ddot{\Gamma}(\mathbf{s}(x))^{\perp}
, respectively.

We consider an initial vorticity for (
1.1
) which is given as an infinite superposition of almost parallel vortex sheets. To make this precise, we start by choosing a family of analytic functions
(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{0})\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}
depending on a small parameter
\varepsilon>0
, which we will eventually use to ensure that our initial vorticity concentrates around the curve
\Gamma
. Then, we define the functions

(2.6)

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s):=\varepsilon[l+\eta_{\varepsilon}^{0}(s)]\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}\,,

which we parametrize by a real variable
l\in[-1,1]
, and use them to define a parametrized family of analytic curves as

(2.7)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s):=\Gamma(s)+\nu_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s)\dot{\Gamma}(s)^{\perp}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}^{2}\,.

Next, we take another parametrized family of analytic functions
(\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0})_{l\in[-1,+1]}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}
, which will play the role of vorticity intensities in our construction. We want this family to have compact support in the parameter
l
, so we impose that
\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}\equiv 0
for
|l|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Concerning the dependence on the parameter
l
, we shall assume that
l\mapsto\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}
is in
C^{0}((-1,1),{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}})
.

Having this family of curves and intensities at hand, we consider an initial datum of the form

(2.8)

\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}(x):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\omega}_{\varepsilon}^{0}(\mathbf{s}(x),\mathbf{n}(x))&\text{if }x\in U_{\Gamma}\,,\\
0&\text{if }x\not\in U_{\Gamma}\,,\end{cases}

where
\widetilde{\omega}_{\varepsilon}^{0}
is such that for any test function
\Phi\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, one has

(2.9)

\langle\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0},\Phi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s)\Phi(\mathbf{x}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s))){\,\mathrm{d}}s{\,\mathrm{d}}l\,.

It is straightforward to check that
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}\in C_{c}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and that for small enough
\varepsilon
, its support is contained in a thin tubular neighborhood of the curve
\Gamma
:

(2.10)

\operatorname{supp}\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}\subset\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mathrm{dist}(x,\Gamma)\leqslant\varepsilon\Big(1+\|\eta_{\varepsilon}^{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\right\}\,.

Moreover,
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}
is as regular as the dependence on
l
allows. In other words, if we assume that
\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}
is a
C^{r}
functions of
l
for some
0\leqslant r\leqslant\infty
, the initial datum
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}
given in (
2.8
) belongs to
C_{c}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Yudovich’s theory (see
[
41
]
or
[
1
, Theorem 7.27]
) guarantees the existence and uniqueness of weak solutions to (
1.1
) for any initial vorticity in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Moreover, if the initial vorticity is in
C^{r}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for some
1\leqslant r\leqslant\infty
, then the unique solution to the Euler equation (
1.1
) is in
C_{w}([0,+\infty),C_{c}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
(where
C_{w}
denotes the space of functions which are weakly continuous). Although the arguments we will use work directly with weaker regularity in the variable
l
, we are primarily interested in the case where
\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}
is a smooth function of
l
, which ensures that the vorticity is a
C^{\infty}
function of
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
t\in[0,+\infty)
.

For any initial vorticity
\omega_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the unique solution to (
1.1
) is given by
[
41
]

\omega(x,t):=\omega_{0}(X^{-1}_{t}(x))\,,

where
X_{t}^{-1}
denotes the inverse of the particle-trajectory map at time
t
, namely the homeomorphism
X_{t}(x):=X(x,t)
defined by the value at time
t
of the unique solution to the ODE

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}X(x,t)=u(X(x,t),t)\,,\qquad X(x,0)=x\,.

Thus, if we denote by
\omega_{\varepsilon}
the unique solution to (
1.1
) with initial datum
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}
given in (
2.8
), then

(2.11)

\displaystyle\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}(X^{-1}_{t}(x))\,.

Therefore, the support of
\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
is contained in the set

\big\{X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s)):s\in\mathbb{T},\;l\in[-1,1]\big\}\,.

Note that, since the velocity field
u_{\varepsilon}:=K*\omega_{\varepsilon}
is log-Lipschitz, for small enough
\varepsilon
, the image of the curve
\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}
under the particle-trajectory map
X_{t}
is contained in the tubular neighborhood
U_{\Gamma}
for any sufficiently short time.

We can now assume that, for any small enough
t
and each
s\in\mathbb{T}
, there exists a unique point
\sigma_{t}(s)\in\mathbb{T}
(depending on
l
) satisfying

(2.12)

\mathbf{s}(X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))))=s\,.

We shall assume that the map
\sigma_{t}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}
is a homeomorphism for each small enough
t
. Geometrically, this assumption amounts to saying that the transported curve
X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0})
remains a graph over the reference curve
\Gamma
(which will be an immediate consequence of the equations for small enough times). Note that
\sigma_{0}
is the identity.

We reparametrize the transported curves as

(2.13)

\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t):=X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\,.

Similarly as in the case of the family of initial curves, let us introduce the notation

(2.14)

\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t):=\mathbf{n}(X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))))

for the
n
-coordinate of these curves, which we will eventually write as

(2.15)

\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)-\nu_{\varepsilon,\ell}(s,t):=\varepsilon\int_{\ell}^{l}\big(1+\partial_{\mu}\eta_{\varepsilon,\mu}(s,t)\big){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu\,.

In terms of this family of evolved curves, it is natural to make the ansatz that the vorticity (
2.11
) at a small enough time
t
can be written as

(2.16)

\omega_{\varepsilon}(x,t):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{s}(x),\mathbf{n}(x),t)&\text{if }x\in U_{\Gamma}\,,\\
0&\text{if }x\not\in U_{\Gamma}\,,\end{cases}

with

(2.17)

\widetilde{\omega}_{\varepsilon}(s,n,t):=\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\,\delta(n-\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))\,\mathrm{d}l\,.

The vorticity should be interpreted as the compactly supported time-dependent distribution that acts on
\Phi\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as

(2.18)

\langle\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t),\Phi\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\,\Phi(\mathbf{x}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)))\,\mathrm{d}l\,\mathrm{d}s\,.

Let us also stress here that, by continuity, the structure (
2.13
)–(
2.17
) for
\omega_{\varepsilon}
will also persist until one of the quantities
\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(\cdot,t),\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(\cdot,t)
is not in
C^{0}(\mathbb{T})
anymore.

Of course, the vorticity intensity
\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}
at time 0 must satisfy

(2.19)

\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(s,0)=\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s)

and, by definition,

(2.20)

\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,0)=\nu_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s)\,,\qquad\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,0)=\eta^{0}_{\varepsilon}(s)\,.

Having this notation and this geometric setting at hand, we can now give a more precise version of our main result. Here and in what follows, we will say that a quantity
\mu_{l}(s)
is in the space
C^{k}\mathcal{{\mathcal{X}}}_{\rho}
with
\rho>0
and
k
a nonnegative integer if
l\mapsto\mu_{l}(\cdot)
is in
C^{k}((-1,1),\mathcal{{\mathcal{X}}}_{\rho})
and

\|\mu_{l}\|_{C^{k}\mathcal{{\mathcal{X}}}_{\rho}}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\|\partial_{l}^{j}\mu_{l}\|_{\rho}<\infty\,.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\rho_{0}>0
be a real number. We fix a reference curve
\Gamma\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}^{2}
satisfying (
2.2
) and
\Gamma^{(5)}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}^{2}
and, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, consider a family of initial data
(\eta_{\varepsilon}^{0})_{\varepsilon}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}
and
(\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0})_{\varepsilon}\subset C^{0}{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}
, with
\varpi^{0}_{\varepsilon,l}\equiv 0
for all
|l|>\frac{1}{2}
, satisfying the uniform bound

C_{0}:=\sup_{0<\varepsilon<1}\left(\,\|\eta_{\varepsilon}^{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}}+\|\dot{\eta}_{\varepsilon}^{0}\|_{{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}}}+\|\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}\|_{C^{0}\mathcal{{\mathcal{X}}}_{\rho_{0}}}\,\right)<\infty\,.

Then, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
such that, for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, there exist a constant
C_{1}>0
and some time
T>0
, depending on
C_{0}
but not on
\varepsilon
, such that, for all
0<\rho<\rho_{0}-C_{1}T
:

(i)

The solution
\omega_{\varepsilon}
to the Euler equation (
1.1
) with initial datum (
2.8
) is of the form (
2.16
), (
2.17
) for all
t\in[0,T)
. In particular,
\omega_{\varepsilon}\in C([0,T),C_{c}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

(ii)

If
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}\in C_{c}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for some
0<r<\infty
, then
\omega_{\varepsilon}\in C_{w}([0,T),C_{c}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

(iii)

If
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, then

\omega_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}([0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

(iv)

\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(\cdot,t)\equiv 0
for all
|l|>\frac{3}{4}
and all
t\in[0,T)
. In particular, for all
t\in[0,T)
, the support of
\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
is contained in a tubular neighborhood of
\Gamma
of width
C_{2}\varepsilon
, for some
C_{2}>0
depending on
C_{0}
, but not on
\varepsilon
.

(v)

If we assume that

(2.21)

\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}{\,\mathrm{d}}l\to\varpi_{0}^{0}\quad\textup{in }{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho}\,,\quad\textup{as }\varepsilon\to 0^{+}\,,

and denote by
(\nu_{0},\varpi_{0})
the unique solution to the Birkhoff–Rott equations (written as a graph over the curve
\Gamma
as in (
3.7
)) with initial datum
(0,\varpi_{0}^{0})
, there exists some time
T^{\prime}\in(0,T]
, depending on
C_{0}
but not on
\varepsilon
, such that, for all
0<\rho^{\prime}<\rho
and all
t\in[0,T^{\prime})
,

(2.22)

\hskip 42.67912pt\left\lVert\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(\cdot,t)-\nu_{0}(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}+\left\lVert\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(\cdot,t){\,\mathrm{d}}l-\varpi_{0}(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\varepsilon+\left\lVert\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}{\,\mathrm{d}}l-\varpi_{0}^{0}\right\rVert_{\rho}\,.

In particular, setting

(2.23)

\omega_{\rm sing}(x,t):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\widetilde{\omega}_{0}(\mathbf{s}(x),\mathbf{n}(x),t)&&\text{if }x\in U_{\Gamma}\,,\\
&0&&\text{if }x\not\in U_{\Gamma}\,,\end{aligned}\right.

with

\widetilde{\omega}_{0}(s,n,t):=\varpi_{0}(s,t)\,\delta(n-\nu_{0}(s,t))\,,

it follows that, for all
t\in[0,T^{\prime})
,

\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\to\omega_{\rm sing}(\cdot,t)\,,\quad\textup{as }\varepsilon\to 0^{+}\,,

in the sense of distributions.

Remark 2.2
.

Our proof also works if we only assume that
\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}\equiv 0
for
|l|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
and that
l\mapsto\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}
is in
L^{\infty}((-1,1),{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}})
instead of in
C^{0}((-1,1),{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho_{0}})
. This allows us to recover Benedetto and Pulvirenti’s result
[
2
]
on desingularization of the vortex sheet via vortex patches, without any of their assumptions on the geometry of the sheet.

3.
Proof of the main result

In this section we present the proof of Theorem
2.1
. To streamline the presentation, we will state several key auxiliary results whose proofs are relegated to later sections.

Step 1: Derivation of an effective evolution equation

As a first step towards the proof of Theorem
2.1
, we shall derive the evolution equations for
\nu_{\varepsilon,l}
,
\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon,l}
and
\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}
(see (
2.14
)–(
2.17
)). To do so, let us introduce some more notation. First, it is convenient to consider the expression of the velocity field
u_{\varepsilon}:=K*\omega_{\varepsilon}
inside the tubular neighborhood
U_{\Gamma}
in terms of the coordinates
(s,n)
, introduced in (
2.3
). We therefore set

(3.1)

\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,n,t):=u_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}(s,n),t)\,.

Likewise, we define the orthogonal basis

\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,n):=e_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{x}(s,n))\,,\quad\textup{and}\quad\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s,n):=e_{\mathbf{n}}(\mathbf{x}(s,n))\,.

Note that the unit field
\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s,n)
is independent of
n
, so henceforth we will simply write
\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s)
. Now, let

(3.2)

\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,n,t):=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,n,t)\cdot\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,n)}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,n)|^{2}}\,,\qquad\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,n,t):=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,n,t)\cdot{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s)}

denote the components of the velocity field in this basis, in terms of which one can write

\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,n,t)=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,n,t)\,\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,n)+\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,n,t)\,\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s)\,.

We are now ready to give the system of evolution equations that
\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)
and
\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)
must satisfy for (
2.11
) to be a solution to the Euler equations. The proof of this result is postponed to Section
4
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let

(3.3)

\kappa(s,n):=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,n)}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,n)|^{2}}\Big)\,.

Assume that the solution
\omega_{\varepsilon}
to the Euler equations has the structure (
2.13
)–(
2.17
) and that

\left\lVert\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon,l}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times[0,T))}<1

for all
|l|\leqslant 1
. Then, for
s\in\mathbb{T}
:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\textup{\rm a) }\ \partial_{t}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)+\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\,\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\,,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\textup{\rm b) }\ \partial_{t}\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)+\partial_{s}\Big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\big(1+\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.55461pt=\big(\kappa(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))\cdot\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\big)\big(1+\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)\,,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\textup{\rm c) }\ \partial_{t}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)+\partial_{s}\big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)=0\,.

Having this result at hand, and before proceeding further, let us point out that, by arguing as in the proof of Proposition
3.1
, one can verify that the Birkhoff–Rott equations, when written as a graph over the curve
\Gamma
, read as

(3.7)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\nu_{0}(s,t)+\widetilde{u}_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{0}(s,t),t)\,\partial_{s}\nu_{0}(s,t)=\widetilde{u}_{0}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\nu_{0}(s,t),t)\,,\\
&\partial_{t}\varpi_{0}(s,t)+\partial_{s}\big(\widetilde{u}_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{0}(s,t),t)\varpi_{0}(s,t)\big)=0\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Here the vortex sheet
\omega_{0}
is written as

(3.8)

\omega_{0}(x,t):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\omega}_{0}(\mathbf{s}(x),\mathbf{n}(x),t)&\text{if }x\in U_{\Gamma}\,,\\
0&\text{if }x\not\in U_{\Gamma}\,,\end{cases}

with

(3.9)

\widetilde{\omega}_{0}(s,n,t):=\varpi_{0}(s,t)\,\delta(n-\nu_{0}(s,t))\,.

Also, note that the velocity
\widetilde{u}_{0}
at the curve
\gamma_{0}(s,t):=\Gamma(s)+\nu_{0}(s,t)\dot{\Gamma}(s)^{\perp}
can be recovered from
\nu_{0}
and
\varpi_{0}
through the Biot–Savart law:

(3.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{0}(s,\nu_{0}(s,t),t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\,{\rm p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\left(\gamma_{0}(s,t)-\gamma_{0}(\varsigma,t)\right)^{\perp}}{|\gamma_{0}(s,t)-\gamma_{0}(\varsigma,t)|^{2}}\varpi_{0}(\varsigma,t){\,\mathrm{d}}\varsigma

(3.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}{\rm p.v.}\,\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\left(\gamma_{0}(s,t)-\gamma_{0}(s+\varsigma,t)\right)^{\perp}}{|\gamma_{0}(s,t)-\gamma_{0}(s+\varsigma,t)|^{2}}\varpi_{0}(s+\varsigma,t){\,\mathrm{d}}\varsigma\,.

Step 2: Local existence for the effective system

We aim to prove that the effective equations derived in Proposition
3.1
are locally well-posed. Essentially as a consequence of the Kelvin–Helmholtz instability inherent to vortex sheets, we will need to consider solutions that are analytic in the
s
variable and use a theorem of Cauchy–Kovalevskaya type.

For this, we start by decomposing the velocity
\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)
into the contributions of the individual curves in (
2.16
) by making use of (
2.14
) and (
2.15
). With
s\in\mathbb{T}
, as assumed in the derivation of the effective equations, this amounts to the formula

(3.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)-\gamma_{\varepsilon,\ell}(\varsigma,t))^{\perp}}{|\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)-\gamma_{\varepsilon,\ell}(\varsigma,t)|^{2}}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\ell}(\varsigma,t){\,\mathrm{d}}\varsigma{\,\mathrm{d}}\ell

(3.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)-\gamma_{\varepsilon,\ell}(s+\varsigma,t))^{\perp}}{|\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)-\gamma_{\varepsilon,\ell}(s+\varsigma,t)|^{2}}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\ell}(s+\varsigma,t){\,\mathrm{d}}\varsigma{\,\mathrm{d}}\ell\,.

Given functions
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g,\varpi\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho}
, and under the assumption that
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g
are small enough, let us define the operator

(3.14)

K_{\varepsilon}[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g,\varpi](z):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\Xi_{\varepsilon}[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g](z,\varsigma)^{\perp}}{[\Xi_{1}^{\varepsilon}[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g](z,\varsigma)]^{2}+[\Xi_{2}^{\varepsilon}[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g](z,\varsigma)]^{2}}\varpi(z+\varsigma)\,{\,\mathrm{d}}\varsigma\,.

Here,
\Xi^{\varepsilon}[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g]\equiv(\Xi_{1}^{\varepsilon}[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g],\Xi_{2}^{\varepsilon}[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g])
is defined as

\displaystyle\Xi^{\varepsilon}[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},g](z,\varsigma):=\Gamma(z)-\Gamma(z+\varsigma)+[\nu_{1}(z)-\varepsilon g(z)]\,\dot{\Gamma}(z)^{\perp}-\nu_{1}(z+\varsigma)\dot{\Gamma}(z+\varsigma)^{\perp}\,,

for
z\in\mathbb{T}_{\rho}
and
\varsigma\in\mathbb{T}
.

Extending the action of the operator
K_{\varepsilon}
to functions that depend on
(z,t)
in the obvious way, our next goal is to consider the effective system (
3.4
)–(
3.6
) as an evolution problem for functions that are holomorphic in the variable
z\in\mathbb{T}_{\rho}
. For this purpose, for
z\in\mathbb{T}_{\rho}
one can
define
the field
\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}
appearing in this system as

(3.15)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(z,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(z,t),t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{-1}^{1}K_{\varepsilon}\left[\nu_{\varepsilon,\ell},\,\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon,\ell},\,\int_{l}^{\ell}(1+\partial_{\mu}\eta_{\varepsilon,\mu}){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu,\,\varpi_{\varepsilon,\ell}\right](z,t){\,\mathrm{d}}\ell\,.

By construction, this coincides with (
3.13
) for
z=s\in\mathbb{T}
.

This formulation will allow us to apply Nishida’s Cauchy–Kovalevskaya theorem
[
35
]
to the evolution equations found in Proposition
3.1
. For the benefit of the reader, we state here the precise version of the theorem we are going to use:

Theorem 3.2

(Nishida
[
35
]
)
.

Let
\{Y_{\rho}\}_{\rho\in[0,\infty)}
be a scale of Banach spaces, and let
B^{R}_{\rho}:=\{w\in Y_{\rho}:\|w\|_{Y_{\rho}}<R\}
denote the ball in
Y_{\rho}
of radius
R
. Assume that, for any
0\leqslant\rho^{\prime}<\rho
,
Y_{\rho}
is a linear subspace of
Y_{\rho^{\prime}}
and
\|w\|_{Y_{\rho^{\prime}}}\leqslant\|w\|_{Y_{\rho}}
for all
w\in Y_{\rho}
. Consider the initial value problem

(3.16)

w^{\prime}(t)=F(w(t),t)\,,\qquad w(0)=w_{0}\,,

and assume the following conditions on
F
and
w_{0}
, for some positive numbers
R,\delta,\rho_{0}
:

(i)

For every
0<\rho^{\prime}<\rho<\rho_{0}
, the map
F:B_{\rho}^{R}\times(-\delta,\delta)\to Y_{\rho^{\prime}},\ (w,t)\mapsto F(w,t),
is continuous.

(ii)

For any
0<\rho^{\prime}<\rho<\rho_{0}
and all
w^{1},w^{2}\in B_{\rho}^{R}
, and for any
t\in(-\delta,\delta)
,
F
satisfies

\|F(w^{1},t)-F(w^{2},t)\|_{Y_{\rho^{\prime}}}\leqslant\frac{C}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,\|w^{1}-w^{2}\|_{Y_{\rho}}\,,

where
C>0
is a fixed constant independent of
t,w^{1},w^{2},\rho
or
\rho^{\prime}
.

(iii)

w^{0}\in B_{\rho_{0}}^{R_{0}}
for some
0<R_{0}<R
and
F(w^{0},\cdot):(-\delta,\delta)\to Y_{\rho}
,
\,t\mapsto F(w^{0},t)
, is a continuous function for every
0<\rho<\rho_{0}
. Moreover, it satisfies, with a fixed constant
K>0
,

(3.17)

\sup_{|t|<\delta}\|F(w^{0},t)\|_{Y_{\rho}}\leqslant\frac{K}{\rho_{0}-\rho}.

Under these hypotheses, there is a positive constant
\delta_{0}
(depending only on
R_{0}
,
R
,
C
and
K
) such that there exists a unique function
w(t)
which, for every
0\leqslant\rho<\rho_{0}
, is a continuously differentiable function of
t\in(-(\rho_{0}-\rho)\delta_{0},\,(\rho_{0}-\rho)\delta_{0})
with values in
B_{\rho}^{R}
that satisfies the initial value problem (
3.16
).

As already announced, we would like to apply Theorem
3.2
to solve the evolution equations found in Proposition
3.1
. To that end, we first set

\nu_{\varepsilon}[l,s](t):=\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\,,\quad\eta_{\varepsilon}[l,s](t):=\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\,,\quad\textup{and}\quad\varpi_{\varepsilon}[l,s](t):=\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\,,

and define

(3.18)

\displaystyle w[l,s](t)

\displaystyle=\big(w_{1}[l,s](t),\,w_{2}[l,s](t),\,w_{3}[l,s](t),\,w_{4}[l,s](t)\big)

\displaystyle:=\big(\nu_{\varepsilon}[l,s](t),\,\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon}[l,s](t),\,\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon}[l,s](t),\,\varpi_{\varepsilon}[l,s](t)\big)\,.

Also, we introduce

(3.19)

U_{\varepsilon}\big(w[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,s):=\int_{-1}^{1}K_{\varepsilon}\left[\,w_{1}[\ell,\cdot],\,w_{2}[\ell,\cdot],\,\int_{l}^{\ell}(1+w_{3}[\mu,\cdot]){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu,\,w_{4}[\ell,\cdot]\right](s){\,\mathrm{d}}\ell

and we further define:

(3.20)

\displaystyle\ U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{n}}\big(w[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,s)

\displaystyle:=U_{\varepsilon}\big(w[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,s)\cdot\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s)\,,

\displaystyle U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{s}}\big(w[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,s)

\displaystyle:=U_{\varepsilon}\big(w[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,s)\cdot\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,w_{1}[l,s])}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,w_{1}[l,s])|^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\big(w[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,s)

\displaystyle:=\kappa(s,w_{1}[l,s])\,.

Since
\mathcal{K}\big(w[\cdot,\cdot]\big)
only depends on the first component of
w
, in what follows we will also use the notation

\mathcal{K}\big(w_{1}[\cdot,\cdot]\big)\,.

Considering functions that depend on
[l,s](t)
in the obvious way, we then consider the initial value problem

(3.21)

w^{\prime}(t)=F(w(t),t)\,,\quad w(0)=w^{0}\,,

where

\displaystyle F_{1}(w):=U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{n}}(w)-U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{s}}(w)w_{2}\,,

\displaystyle F_{2}(w):=\partial_{s}\big(U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{n}}(w)-U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{s}}(w)w_{2}\big)\,,

\displaystyle F_{3}(w):=\Big(\mathcal{K}(w)\cdot U_{\varepsilon}(w)\Big)(1+w_{3})-\partial_{s}\Big(U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{s}}(w)(1+w_{3})\Big)\,,

\displaystyle F_{4}(w):=-\partial_{s}\Big(U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{s}}(w)w_{4}\Big)\,,

and where

(3.22)

w^{0}\,[l,s]:=(\nu_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s),\,\dot{\nu}^{0}_{\varepsilon,l}(s),\,0,\,\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s))\,,

so that (
3.21
) is the system introduced in Proposition
3.1
.

We want to use Theorem
3.2
to solve (
3.21
)–(
3.22
). Hence, for
\rho>0
, let us introduce the scale of Banach spaces

{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rho}^{k}:=\bigg\{f:\mathbb{T}_{\rho}\times[-1,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{k}\bigg|\ l\mapsto f_{j}(\cdot,l)\in C([-1,1],{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho})\,\textup{ \& }\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\|f_{j}(\cdot,l)\|_{\rho}<\infty\,,\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant k\bigg\},

endowed with the norm

\|f\|_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rho}^{k}}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\,\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\|f_{j}(\cdot,l)\|_{\rho}\,.

Of course, the variables
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
in (
3.18
) are not independent of each other, and we cannot expect the system to behave well for arbitrary data in
{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho}
. We define the set of admissible data as

\mathcal{Z}_{\rho}:=\left\{w\in{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho}\,\bigg|\,\partial_{s}w_{1}[l,\cdot]=w_{2}[l,\cdot]\textup{ and }w_{1}[l,s](t)-w_{1}[\ell,s](t)=\varepsilon\int_{\ell}^{l}(1+w_{3}[\mu,s](t)){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu,\ \forall\ l,\ell\right\}.

Note that, for every
\rho>0
,
\mathcal{Z}_{\rho}
is a Banach subspace of
{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho}
endowed with the same norm as
{\mathcal{Y}}_{\rho}^{4}.
Moreover, if
w\in\mathcal{Z}_{\rho}
and
F(w)\in{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho^{\prime}}
for some
0<\rho^{\prime}<\rho
, then
F(w)\in\mathcal{Z}_{\rho^{\prime}}
. Indeed, this is trivial for the coupling between
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
, and for the coupling between
w_{1}
and
w_{3}
it follows from the derivation of the equation (
3.5
).

Having this functional setting at hand, in the next two results, whose proofs are postponed to Sections
5
and
6
, we prove the required estimates to verify that (
3.21
) fits the framework of Theorem
3.2
.

Proposition 3.3
.

There exists a constant
\mathcal{C}_{1}>0
, depending only on
\Gamma
, such that, for every
0\leqslant\rho\leqslant\rho_{0}\,,
if
w_{1}^{1},w_{1}^{2}\in{\mathcal{Y}}^{1}_{\rho}
satisfy

\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\Big\{\left\lVert w_{1}^{1}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert w_{1}^{2}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}\Big\}<\mathcal{C}_{1}\,,

then

(3.23)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\bigg\{\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\big(w_{1}^{i}[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert\frac{e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{i}[l,\cdot])}{|e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{i}[l,\cdot])|^{2}}\right\rVert_{\rho}\bigg\}\lesssim 1\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,,

and

(3.24)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\bigg\{

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\big(w_{1}^{1}[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,\cdot)-\mathcal{K}\big(w_{1}^{2}[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho}

(3.25)

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1}[l,\cdot])}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1}[l,\cdot])|^{2}}-\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{2}[l,\cdot])}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{2}[l,\cdot])|^{2}}\right\rVert_{\rho}\bigg\}\lesssim\,\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert w_{1}^{1}[l,\cdot]-w_{1}^{2}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}\,,

with implicit constants depending on
\Gamma
, but not on
\rho
.

Proposition 3.4
.

There exists a constant
\mathcal{C}_{2}>0
, depending only on
\Gamma
, such that, for every
0\leqslant\rho\leqslant\rho_{0}\,,
if
w^{1},w^{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\rho}
satisfy

(3.26)

\sum_{j=1}^{3}\bigg(\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\Big\{\left\lVert w_{j}^{1}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert w_{j}^{2}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}\Big\}\bigg)<\mathcal{C}_{2}\,,\quad\textup{and}\quad\mathcal{C}_{3}:=\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\big\{\left\lVert w_{4}^{1}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert w_{4}^{2}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}\big\}<\infty\,,

then

(3.27)

\displaystyle\sup_{l,\,\ell\in[-1,1],\ l\neq\ell}\,\bigg\|K_{\varepsilon}\bigg[w_{1}^{i}[\ell,\cdot],\,w_{2}^{i}[\ell,\cdot],\,\int_{l}^{\ell}(1+w_{3}^{i}[\mu,\cdot]){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu,\,w_{4}^{i}[\ell,\cdot]\bigg](\cdot)\bigg\|_{\rho}\lesssim 1\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,,\quad

and

(3.28)

\displaystyle\sup_{l,\,\ell\in[-1,1],\ l\neq\ell}\,\bigg\|

\displaystyle K_{\varepsilon}\bigg[w_{1}^{1}[\ell,\cdot],\,w_{2}^{1}[\ell,\cdot],\,\int_{l}^{\ell}(1+w_{3}^{1}[\mu,\cdot]){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu,\,w_{4}^{1}[\ell,\cdot]\bigg](\cdot)

\displaystyle-K_{\varepsilon}\bigg[w_{1}^{2}[\ell,\cdot],\,w_{2}^{2}[\ell,\cdot],\,\int_{l}^{\ell}(1+w_{3}^{2}[\mu,\cdot]){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu,\,w_{4}^{2}[\ell,\cdot]\bigg](\cdot)\bigg\|_{\rho}\lesssim\|w^{1}-w^{2}\|_{{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho}}\,,

with implicit constants depending on
\Gamma
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{3}
, but not on
\rho
.

As an immediate corollary, we also obtain the following estimates.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{2}>0
be as in Proposition
3.4
, depending only on
\Gamma
. For every
0\leqslant\rho\leqslant\rho_{0}\,,
and every
w^{1},w^{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\rho}
satisfying (
3.26
), it follows that

(3.29)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert U_{\varepsilon}\big(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho}\lesssim 1\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,,

and that

(3.30)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert U_{\varepsilon}\big(w^{1}[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,\cdot)-U_{\varepsilon}\big(w^{2}[\cdot,\cdot]\big)(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho}\lesssim\|w^{1}-w^{2}\|_{{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho}}\,.

We can now prove the existence of a solution to (
3.21
) with
w(0)=w^{0}
given in (
3.22
). We set
\mathcal{C}_{0}:=\min\{\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\}
, depending only on
\Gamma
, and, for
0\leqslant\rho\leqslant\rho_{0}\,,
consider
w^{1},w^{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\rho}
satisfying

(3.31)

\sum_{j=1}^{3}\bigg(\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\Big\{\left\lVert w_{j}^{1}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert w_{j}^{2}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}\Big\}\bigg)<\mathcal{C}_{0}\quad\textup{and}\quad\mathcal{C}_{3}:=\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\big\{\left\lVert w_{4}^{1}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert w_{4}^{2}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}\big\}<\infty\,.

First of all, using that
{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho}
is an algebra (since
C^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
is), we see that

(3.32)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{1}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho}

(3.33)

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert U_{\varepsilon}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho}\left(\|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}\|_{\rho}+\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\bigg\{\left\lVert w_{2}^{i}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}\left\lVert\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{i}[l,\cdot])}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{i}[l,\cdot])|^{2}}\right\rVert_{\rho}\bigg\}\right),\ i\in\{1,2\}\,,

and thus, by Proposition
3.3
and Corollary
3.5
we get that

(3.34)

\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{1}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho}\lesssim 1\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,.

On the other hand, observe that

\displaystyle F_{1}(w^{1})-F_{1}(w^{2})=\big(U_{\varepsilon}(w^{1})-U_{\varepsilon}(w^{2})\big)\cdot\left(\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(\cdot)+w_{2}^{2}\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{2})}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{2})|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+(w_{2}^{1}-w_{2}^{2})\,U_{\varepsilon}(w^{1})\cdot\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})|^{2}}+w_{2}^{2}U_{\varepsilon}(w^{1})\cdot\left(\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})|^{2}}-\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{2})}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{2})|^{2}}\right).

Combining Proposition
3.3
with Corollary
3.5
, and using again that
{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho}
is an algebra, we then get

(3.35)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{1}(w^{1}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)-F_{1}(w^{2}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho}\lesssim\|w^{1}-w^{2}\|_{{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho}}\,.

Now, to deal with
F_{2}
, we use that

F_{2}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(s)=\partial_{s}\left(F_{1}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(s)\right)\,.

Indeed, by Cauchy’s integral formula, it follows that

\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{2}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\,\left\lVert F_{1}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho}\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,,

uniformly in
0\leqslant\rho^{\prime}<\rho\leqslant\rho_{0}
. Hence, using (
3.34
) and (
3.35
), we get that

(3.36)

\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{2}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,,

and

(3.37)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{2}(w^{1}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)-F_{2}(w^{2}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,\|w^{1}-w^{2}\|_{{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho}}\,.

Next, observe that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

(3.38)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{3}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\leqslant\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert\partial_{s}\big(U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{s}}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\,(1+w_{3}^{i}[l,\cdot])\big)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}

(3.39)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\Big\{\left\lVert 1+w_{3}^{i}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\,\left\lVert\mathcal{K}(w_{1}^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\left\lVert U_{\varepsilon}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\Big\}\,.

The first term on the right-hand side can be estimated as we did with
F_{2}
. Indeed, combining the Cauchy integral formula with Proposition
3.3
and Corollary
3.5
, we get that,

\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert\partial_{s}\big(U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{s}}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\,(1+w_{3}^{i}[l,\cdot])\big)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,,

uniformly in
0\leqslant\rho^{\prime}<\rho\leqslant\rho_{0}
. The second can be estimated directly by combining Proposition
3.3
with Corollary
3.5
, as we did with
F_{1}
. In short, we get that

(3.40)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{3}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,.

Also, observe that

(3.41)

\displaystyle F_{3}(w^{1})-F_{3}(w^{2})=(w_{3}^{1}-w_{3}^{2})\,\mathcal{K}(w^{1})\cdot U_{\varepsilon}(w^{1})

(3.42)

\displaystyle\quad+(1+w_{3}^{2})\Big(\big(\mathcal{K}(w^{1})-\mathcal{K}(w^{2})\big)\cdot U_{\varepsilon}(w^{1})+\mathcal{K}(w^{2})\cdot\big(U_{\varepsilon}(w^{1})-U_{\varepsilon}(w^{2})\big)\Big)

(3.43)

\displaystyle\quad-\partial_{s}\bigg[(w_{3}^{1}-w_{3}^{2})\,\frac{e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})}{|e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})|^{2}}\cdot U_{\varepsilon}(w^{1})

(3.44)

\displaystyle\hskip 38.41139pt+(1+w_{3}^{2})\bigg(\bigg(\frac{e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})}{|e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})|^{2}}-\frac{e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{2})}{|e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{2})|^{2}}\bigg)\cdot U_{\varepsilon}(w^{1})+\frac{e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})}{|e_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,w_{1}^{1})|^{2}}\cdot\big(U_{\varepsilon}(w^{1})-U_{\varepsilon}(w^{2})\big)\bigg)\bigg]\,.

Having this expression at hand, one can argue as we did to prove (
3.35
) and (
3.37
) and conclude:

(3.45)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{3}(w^{1}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)-F_{3}(w^{2}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,\|w^{1}-w^{2}\|_{{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho}}\,.

Finally, to estimate
F_{4}
, we once again use Cauchy’s integral formula to infer that

\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{4}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert U_{\varepsilon}^{\mathbf{s}}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\,w_{4}^{i}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,,

uniformly in
0\leqslant\rho^{\prime}<\rho\leqslant\rho_{0}
. Arguing exactly as for the previous terms, we conclude that

(3.46)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{4}(w^{i}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,,

and that

(3.47)

\displaystyle\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert F_{4}(w^{1}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)-F_{4}(w^{2}[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\,\|w^{1}-w^{2}\|_{{\mathcal{Y}}^{4}_{\rho}}\,.

Combining (
3.34
), (
3.35
), (
3.36
), (
3.37
), (
3.40
), (
3.45
), (
3.46
) and (
3.47
), it is straightforward to check the assumptions of Theorem
3.2
. Thus, under suitable assumptions on the initial data
w(0)=w^{0}
given in (
3.22
), we obtain a unique solution
w(t)
to (
3.21
), for a time of existence
independent of
\varepsilon
. This immediately follows from Theorem
3.2
and the uniform estimates we just proved.

We finally note that the existence of a continuous solution to the system (
3.4
)–(
3.6
), which we just proved, also implies that the solution
\omega_{\varepsilon}
has the desired structure (
2.13
)–(
2.17
). Indeed, this must hold on some non-empty time-interval by mere continuity and the only way
\omega_{\varepsilon}
can lose this structure is if one of
\nu_{\varepsilon,l},\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}
blows up, which is not the case on
[0,T)
, as we have shown.

We have now concluded the proof of Theorem
2.1
(i). Also, note that (ii), (iii) and (iv) are immediate once we have shown the solution has the desired structure. Hence, we now focus on Theorem
2.1
(v).

Step 3: Convergence to the Birkhoff–Rott equations

First, let us recall that the Birkhoff–Rott equations written as a graph over the curve
\Gamma
were introduced in (
3.7
). Moreover, taking into account (
3.11
), for
\nu_{1},\varpi\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho}
, and under the assumption that
\nu_{1}
is small enough, let us set

(3.48)

K_{0}[\nu_{1},\varpi](z):=\frac{1}{2\pi}{\rm p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\Xi^{0}[\nu_{1}](z,\varsigma)^{\perp}}{[\Xi_{1}^{0}[\nu_{1}](z,\varsigma)]^{2}+[\Xi_{2}^{0}[\nu_{1}](z,\varsigma)]^{2}}\,\varpi(z+\varsigma){\,\mathrm{d}}\varsigma\,.

Here,
\Xi^{0}[\nu_{1}]=(\Xi_{1}^{0}[\nu_{1}],\Xi_{2}^{0}[\nu_{1}])
is defined as

\Xi^{0}[\nu_{1}](z,\varsigma):=\Gamma(z)-\Gamma(z+\varsigma)+\nu_{1}(z)\dot{\Gamma}(z)^{\perp}-\nu_{1}(z+\varsigma)\dot{\Gamma}(z+\varsigma)^{\perp}\,,\quad\textup{for }z\in\mathbb{T}_{\rho}\textup{ and }\varsigma\in\mathbb{T}\,.

For later purposes, we also write

(3.49)

\displaystyle U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{n}}((w_{1},w_{4})):=K_{0}[w_{1},w_{4}]\cdot\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}\,,

(3.50)

\displaystyle U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}((w_{1},w_{4})):=K_{0}[w_{1},w_{4}]\cdot\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,w_{1}(\cdot))}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,w_{1}(\cdot))|^{2}}\,.

Now, we denote by

(3.51)

\displaystyle w^{\varepsilon}[l,s](t)=\big(\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)\in\mathcal{Z}_{\rho}\,,

the solution to (
3.21
) obtained in the previous subsection, and denote by

w^{0}(s,t):=(\nu_{0}(s,t),\,\varpi_{0}(s,t))\,,

the unique solution to (
3.7
) with initial datum
(0,\varpi_{0}^{0})
. Likewise, we set

(3.52)

\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nu_{\varepsilon,-1}+\nu_{\varepsilon,1}\right)\,,

and

(3.53)

\displaystyle\bar{w}^{\varepsilon}(s,t)=\left(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(s,t),\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t){\,\mathrm{d}}l\right)\,.

Before going further, let us stress that

(3.54)

\|\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(\cdot,t)-\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)\|_{\rho}\leqslant 4\varepsilon\Big(1+\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(\cdot,t)\right\rVert_{\rho}\Big)\lesssim\varepsilon\,,\quad\textup{for all }t\in[0,T)\,.

Hence, to control the difference
\nu_{0}(\cdot,t)-\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(\cdot,t)
for all
l\in[-1,1]
and all
t\in[0,T)
, it is enough to control the difference
\nu_{0}(\cdot,t)-\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
.

Now, taking into account (
3.6
) and (
3.7
), we get that

(3.55)

\partial_{t}(\nu_{0}-\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon})+E_{1}^{\varepsilon}(w^{0},\bar{w}^{\varepsilon},t)+E_{2}^{\varepsilon}(t)=0\,,

with

(3.56)

\displaystyle\bullet\ E_{1}^{\varepsilon}(w^{0},\bar{w}^{\varepsilon},t):=U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(w^{0})\partial_{s}(\nu_{0}-\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon})+(U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(w^{0})-U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(\bar{w}^{\varepsilon}))\partial_{s}\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}-\left(U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(w^{0})-U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(\bar{w}^{\varepsilon})\right)\,,

(3.57)

\displaystyle\bullet\ E_{2}^{\varepsilon}(s,t):=\left(U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(\bar{w}^{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{2}\big(U_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(w^{\varepsilon})(1,s)+U_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(w^{\varepsilon})(-1,s)\big)\right)\partial_{s}\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}

(3.58)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\left(U_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(w^{\varepsilon})(-1,s)-U_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(w^{\varepsilon})(1,s)\right)\partial_{s}(\nu_{\varepsilon,1}-\nu_{\varepsilon,-1})

(3.59)

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(\bar{w}^{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{2}\left(U_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(w^{\varepsilon})(1,s)+U_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(w^{\varepsilon})(-1,s)\right)\right)\,.

Likewise, it follows that

(3.60)

\partial_{t}\partial_{s}(\nu_{0}-\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon})+\partial_{s}E_{1}^{\varepsilon}(w^{0}-\bar{w}^{\varepsilon},t)+\partial_{s}E_{2}^{\varepsilon}(t)=0\,.

On the other hand, taking into account (
3.6
) and (
3.7
), we get that

(3.61)

\partial_{t}\left(\varpi_{0}-\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}{\,\mathrm{d}}l\right)+E_{3}^{\varepsilon}(w^{0},\bar{w}^{\varepsilon},t)+E_{4}^{\varepsilon}(t)=0\,,

with

(3.62)

\displaystyle\bullet\ E_{3}^{\varepsilon}(w^{0},\bar{w}^{\varepsilon},t):=\partial_{s}\left(U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(w^{0})\left(\varpi_{0}-\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}{\,\mathrm{d}}l\right)\right)+\partial_{s}\left(\left(U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(w^{0})-U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(\bar{w}^{\varepsilon})\right)\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}{\,\mathrm{d}}l\right)\,,

(3.63)

\displaystyle\bullet\ E_{4}^{\varepsilon}(s,t):=\partial_{s}\left(U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(\bar{w}^{\varepsilon})\int_{-1}^{1}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}{\,\mathrm{d}}l-\int_{-1}^{1}U_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(w_{\varepsilon})\,\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}{\,\mathrm{d}}l\right).

Having (
3.55
), (
3.60
) and (
3.61
) at hand, we will use a Gronwall-type argument to prove Theorem
2.1
(v).

In the next two results, whose proofs are postponed to Section
7
, we prove the estimates required to perform the Gronwall-type argument. First, we show that the
\varepsilon
-regularized velocity
U_{\varepsilon}
, defined in (
3.19
), converges to the velocity in (
3.48
), up to the expected jump discontinuity of the vortex sheet. For the identification of the sign of the jump, it is necessary to fix the orientation of
\Gamma
. We assume that
\Gamma
is oriented so that
\dot{\Gamma}^{\perp}
points inside the domain enclosed by
\Gamma
. The other case can be handled by arguing in a similar way.

Proposition 3.6
.

There exists a constant
{\mathcal{C}}_{4}>0
, depending only on
\Gamma
, such that, for every
0\leqslant\rho<\rho_{0}
, if
w\in\mathcal{Z}_{\rho}
satisfies

\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert w_{j}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}<{\mathcal{C}}_{4}\quad\textup{and}\quad{\mathcal{C}}_{5}:=\sup_{|l|\leqslant 1}\left\lVert w_{4}[l,\cdot]\right\rVert_{\rho}<\infty\,,

then, for all
0\leqslant\rho^{\prime}<\rho
, and all
l\in[-1,1]
, it follows that

(3.64)

\displaystyle\bigg\|

\displaystyle\,U_{\varepsilon}(w[\cdot,\cdot])(l,\cdot)-K_{0}\left[\frac{w_{1}[1,\cdot]+w_{1}[-1,\cdot]}{2},\int_{-1}^{1}w_{4}[\mu,\cdot]{\,\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]

(3.65)

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma_{\varepsilon,0}^{\prime}(\cdot)}{2|\gamma_{\varepsilon,0}^{\prime}(\cdot)|^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\mathds{1}_{\mu\geqslant l}-\mathds{1}_{\mu\leqslant l})w_{4}[\mu,\cdot]\right){\,\mathrm{d}}\mu\,\bigg\|_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\varepsilon A(\rho-\rho^{\prime})\,,

and that

(3.66)

\displaystyle\bigg\|

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\Big(U_{\varepsilon}(w[\cdot,\cdot])(1,\cdot)+U_{\varepsilon}(w[\cdot,\cdot])(-1,\cdot)\Big)

(3.67)

\displaystyle-K_{0}\left[\frac{w_{1}[1,\cdot]+w_{1}[-1,\cdot]}{2},\int_{-1}^{1}w_{4}[\mu,\cdot]{\,\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\bigg\|_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\varepsilon A(\rho-\rho^{\prime})\,,

Here,
A:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
is some positive, decreasing function and the implicit constants depend on
\mathcal{C}_{5}
.

Remark 3.7
.

It is natural to lose regularity here. Roughly speaking, quantitative estimates for the difference between the velocity at different curves correspond to taking a normal derivative of the velocity at the curves.
Furthermore, it is also natural not to have a convergence of
U_{\varepsilon}
to
K_{0}
without an additional correction term, because the limit of the velocity should contain the jump of the vortex sheet.

The second result we require is the analogue of Proposition
3.4
and Corollary
3.5
for
K_{0}
. To simplify the notation, we set

{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho}:=\Big\{f=(f_{1},f_{2})\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho}^{2}\ \big|\ \partial_{s}f_{1}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\rho}\Big\}\,.

We regard
{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho}
as a Banach space, equipped with the norm

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho}}:=\|f_{1}\|_{\rho}+\|\partial_{s}f_{1}\|_{\rho}+\|f_{2}\|_{\rho}\,.

Lemma 3.8
.

There exists a constant
\mathcal{C}_{6}>0
, depending only on
\Gamma
, such that, for every
0\leqslant\rho<\rho_{0}
, if
w^{1},w^{2}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho}
satisfy

\left\lVert w_{1}^{1}\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert\partial_{s}w_{1}^{1}\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert w_{1}^{2}\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert\partial_{s}w_{1}^{2}\right\rVert_{\rho}<\mathcal{C}_{6}\quad\textup{and}\quad\mathcal{C}_{7}:=\left\lVert w_{2}^{1}\right\rVert_{\rho}+\left\lVert w_{2}^{2}\right\rVert_{\rho}<\infty\,,

then

(3.68)

\displaystyle\left\|K_{0}[w^{i}]\right\|_{\rho}\lesssim 1\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,,

and

(3.69)

\left\|K_{0}[w^{1}]-K_{0}[w^{2}]\right\|_{\rho}\lesssim\left\lVert w^{1}-w^{2}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho}}\,.

The analogous estimates also hold for
U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{n}}
and
U_{0}^{\,\mathbf{s}}
.

Having these two results at hand, and also Propositions
3.3
and
3.4
, it is straightforward to prove that

(3.70)

\left\lVert E_{1}^{\varepsilon}(w^{0},\bar{w}^{\varepsilon})\right\rVert_{\rho}\lesssim\left\lVert w^{0}-\bar{w}^{\varepsilon}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho}},

and that, for all
0<\rho^{\prime}<\rho
,

(3.71)

\|E_{2}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\varepsilon A(\rho-\rho^{\prime})\,.

Note that, here and during the rest of the proof, we omit the dependence on
t
for the ease of writing.

Likewise, combining (
3.70
) and (
3.71
) with Cauchy’s integral theorem, we get that

\left\lVert\partial_{s}E_{1}^{\varepsilon}(w^{0},\bar{w}^{\varepsilon})\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\left\lVert w^{0}-\bar{w}^{\varepsilon}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho}},

and that

\|\partial_{s}E_{2}^{\varepsilon}\|_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\varepsilon A\Big(\frac{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}{2}\Big)\,,

for all
0<\rho^{\prime}<\rho
.

Finally, arguing similarly, we get that

(3.72)

\left\lVert E_{3}^{\varepsilon}(w^{0},\bar{w}^{\varepsilon})\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\left\lVert w^{0}-\bar{w}^{\varepsilon}\right\rVert_{{\mathcal{W}}_{\rho}},\quad\textup{for all }0<\rho^{\prime}<\rho\,.

Moreover, taking into account that

\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}(\mathds{1}_{\mu\geqslant l}-\mathds{1}_{\mu\leqslant l})\varpi_{\varepsilon,\mu}\varpi_{\varepsilon,l}{\,\mathrm{d}}l{\,\mathrm{d}}\mu=0\,,

and Proposition
3.6
, we see that

(3.73)

\left\lVert E_{4}^{\varepsilon}\right\rVert_{\rho^{\prime}}\lesssim\varepsilon A\Big(\frac{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}{2}\Big),\quad\textup{for all }0<\rho^{\prime}<\rho\,.

Once we have the estimates for
E_{j}^{\varepsilon}
, namely (
3.70
), (
3.71
), (
3.72
) and (
3.73
), Theorem
2.1
(v) follows from a standard Gronwall-type argument. See, for instance,
[
2
, Sect. 3]
or
[
3
, Sect. 10]
for two slightly different approaches to the same conclusion.

Remark 3.9
.

By (
2.21
), we have shown that
\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
converges distributionally to some
\omega^{0}(\cdot,t)
for all
t\in[0,T_{0})
, as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. The weaker result that this happens along some sequence
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0^{+}
is easier, as one can argue directly as in
[
2
, Sect. 2]
.

4.
Vortex sheet-type equations

This section is devoted to the proof of the Proposition
3.1
. We keep here the notation introduced in Sections
2
and
3
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

We derive each equation separately.

a)
Let
X_{t}
be the particle-trajectory map at time
t
associated to the velocity field
u_{\varepsilon}
. For simplicity, here we assume that
u_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)
is
C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The general case for a log-Lipschitz velocity field
u_{\varepsilon}
, which is actually what we have, follows from a standard approximation argument.

Taking into account (
2.12
) and (
2.13
), we get that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\,\big(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\,\Big(X_{t}\big(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=u_{\varepsilon}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)+\partial_{t}\sigma_{t}(s)\,\Big({\rm D}X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)+\partial_{t}\sigma_{t}(s)\,\Big({\rm D}X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\Big)\,,

and so that

(4.1)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\big(\mathbf{s}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))\big)=\nabla\mathbf{s}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\,\big(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)+\partial_{t}\sigma_{t}(s)\,\Big({\rm D}X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\Big)\cdot\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))|^{2}}\,.

Note that here we are using the identity
\mathbf{s}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))=s
. Likewise, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\,\big(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\,\Big(X_{t}\big(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\big)\Big)=\partial_{s}\sigma_{t}(s)\,\Big({\rm D}X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\Big)\,,

and so the identity
\mathbf{s}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))=s
yields

(4.2)

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\big(\mathbf{s}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))\big)=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\,\big(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)\cdot\nabla\mathbf{s}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))

\displaystyle=\partial_{s}\sigma_{t}(s)\,\Big({\rm D}X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\Big)\cdot\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))|^{2}}\,,\quad i\in\{1,2\}\,.

Rearranging (
4.1
) and (
4.2
), we see that

(4.3)

\partial_{t}\sigma_{t}(s)+\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\partial_{s}\sigma_{t}(s)=0\,.

Finally, using (
2.15
), we see that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\big(\mathbf{n}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\big(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)\cdot\nabla\mathbf{n}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))

\displaystyle=\bigg(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)+\partial_{t}\sigma_{t}(s)\,\Big({\rm D}X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\Big)\bigg)\cdot\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)+\partial_{t}\sigma_{t}(s)\Big({\rm D}X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\Big)\cdot\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s)\,,

and that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\big(\mathbf{n}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\big(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\big)\cdot\nabla\mathbf{n}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))

\displaystyle=\partial_{s}\sigma_{t}(s)\,\Big({\rm D}X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\Big)\cdot\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s)\,.

Now, from those two equations and (
4.3
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)+\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)

\displaystyle\quad=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)+\bigl(\partial_{t}\sigma_{t}(s)+\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\partial_{s}\sigma_{t}(s)\bigr)\left(\left(\mathrm{D}X_{t}(\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s)))\dot{\gamma}_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(\sigma_{t}(s))\right)\cdot\tilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s)\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\,,

which is exactly (
3.4
).

b)
Let
l,l^{\prime}\in[-1,1]
be fixed but arbitrary. By (
3.4
) and direct computations, we get that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\big(\eta_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)-\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\Big(\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)-\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\Big)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t),t)-\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t),t)\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)-\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\Big)\,.

Now, on one hand, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\left(\int_{\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)}^{\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)}\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\tau,t)\,{\,\mathrm{d}}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)}^{\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\tau,t)\big){\,\mathrm{d}}\tau+\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t),t)\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)-\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\partial_{s}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)\,,

and on the other hand, that

\displaystyle\int_{\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)}^{\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\tau,t)\big){\,\mathrm{d}}\tau=\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t),t)-\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t),t)\,.

Hence, it follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\big(\eta_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)-\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))

\displaystyle\qquad=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\left(\int_{\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)}^{\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)}\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\tau,t)\,{\,\mathrm{d}}\tau\right)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)}^{\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)}\bigg[\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\tau,t)\big)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\tau,t)\big)\bigg]{\,\mathrm{d}}\tau\,.

Also, using the incompressibility of the velocity field
u_{\varepsilon}
, we infer that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\tau,t)\big)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\big(\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{n}}(s,\tau,t)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\partial_{s}\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\tau,t)\cdot\frac{\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,\tau)}{|\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{s}}(s,\tau)|^{2}}+\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\tau,t)\cdot\widetilde{e}_{\mathbf{n}}(s)+\kappa(s,\tau)\cdot\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\tau,t)

\displaystyle\qquad=\operatorname{div}_{x}u_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}(s,\tau),t)+\kappa(s,\tau)\cdot\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\tau,t)=\kappa(s,\tau)\cdot\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\tau,t)\,,

and therefore

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\big(\eta_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)-\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))

\displaystyle\quad=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\left(\int_{\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)}^{\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)}\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\tau,t)\,{\,\mathrm{d}}\tau\right)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)}^{\nu_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)}\kappa(s,\tau)\cdot\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\tau,t)\,{\,\mathrm{d}}\tau\,.

Next, observe that
\partial_{l}\nu_{\varepsilon,l}=\varepsilon(1+\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon,l})
. In particular, the map
l\mapsto\nu_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t)
is invertible whenever
\|\partial_{l}\eta_{\varepsilon,l}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times[0,T))}<1
. Thus, doing a change of variables in each integral, we get that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\big(\eta_{\varepsilon,l^{\prime}}(s,t)-\eta_{\varepsilon,l}(s,t))=-\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\left(\int_{l}^{l^{\prime}}\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\,\mathbf{s}}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,\tau}(s,t),t)\big(1+\partial_{\tau}\eta_{\varepsilon,\tau}(s,t)\big)\,{\,\mathrm{d}}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{l}^{l^{\prime}}\big(\kappa(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,\tau}(s,t))\cdot\widetilde{u}_{\varepsilon}(s,\nu_{\varepsilon,\tau}(s,t),t)\big)\big(1+\partial_{\tau}\eta_{\varepsilon,\tau}(s,t)\big)\,{\,\mathrm{d}}\tau\,,

for all
l,l^{\prime}\in[-1,1]
. Having this identity at hand, (
3.5
) follows dividing both sides by
l^{\prime}-l
with
l\neq l^{\prime}
, and then sending
l^{\prime}-l
to
0
and using the Lebesgue differentiation theorem on each integral of the right hand side.

c)
Let
X_{t}
be the particle-trajectory map at time
t
associated with the velocity field
u_{\varepsilon}
, and consider a test function
\Phi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
of the form

(4.6)

\Phi(x):=\mathds{1}_{A(a,b,l_{1},l_{2})}(x)\,.

with

(4.7)

A(a,b,l_{1},l_{2}):=\bigcup_{l\in[l_{1},l_{2}]}\bigl\{\gamma_{\varepsilon,l}^{0}(s)\,|\,s\in[a,b]\bigr\}\,.

Here,
\mathds{1}_{A}
denotes the indicator function of a subset
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By the incompressibility of the flow,
